From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Island country in the Pacific Ocean

This article is about the country. For other uses, see [New Zealand (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_(disambiguation) "New Zealand (disambiguation)")."NZ" redirects here. For other uses, see [NZ (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NZ_(disambiguation) "NZ (disambiguation)").

**New Zealand** ( [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language"): _[Aotearoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aotearoa "Aotearoa")_, pronounced[\[aɔˈtɛaɾɔa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/M%C4%81ori "Help:IPA/Māori")[Play audio](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/00/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav "File:Aotearoa Pronunciation.wav")) is an [island country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_country "Island country") in the southwestern [Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean "Pacific Ocean"). It comprises two main landmasses—the [North Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Island "North Island") (_Te Ika-a-Māui_) and the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island "South Island") (_Te Waipounamu_)—and [over 700 smaller islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_New_Zealand "List of islands of New Zealand"). It is the [sixth-largest island country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_island_countries "List of island countries") by area and lies east of [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia "Australia") across the [Tasman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Sea "Tasman Sea") and south of the islands of [New Caledonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Caledonia "New Caledonia"), [Fiji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiji "Fiji"), and [Tonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonga "Tonga"). The [country's varied topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_New_Zealand "Geography of New Zealand") and sharp mountain peaks, including the [Southern Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alps "Southern Alps") (_Kā Tiritiri o te Moana_), owe much to [tectonic uplift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tectonic_uplift "Tectonic uplift") and volcanic eruptions. [New Zealand's capital city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_of_New_Zealand "Capital of New Zealand") is [Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington "Wellington"), and its most populous city is [Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland "Auckland").

The islands of New Zealand were the last large habitable land to be settled by humans. Between about 1280 and 1350, [Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians "Polynesians") began to settle in the islands and subsequently developed a distinctive [Māori culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_culture "Māori culture"). In 1642, the Dutch explorer [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman "Abel Tasman") became the first European to sight and record New Zealand. In 1769, the British explorer [Captain James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook "James Cook") became the first European to set foot on and map New Zealand. In 1840, representatives of the United Kingdom and [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people "Māori people") chiefs signed the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi "Treaty of Waitangi"), which paved the way for Britain's declaration of sovereignty later that year and the establishment of the [Colony of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_Zealand "Colony of New Zealand") in 1841. Subsequently, [a series of conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Wars "New Zealand Wars") between the colonial government and [Māori tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi "Iwi") resulted in the [alienation and confiscation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_land_confiscations "New Zealand land confiscations") of large amounts of Māori land. New Zealand [became a dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_Zealand "Dominion of New Zealand") in 1907; it [gained full statutory independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_New_Zealand "Independence of New Zealand") in 1947, retaining the monarch as [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state "Head of state"). Today, the majority of [New Zealand's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealanders "New Zealanders") of around 5.3million is of [European descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_New_Zealanders "European New Zealanders"); the indigenous Māori are the largest minority, followed by [Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_New_Zealanders "Asian New Zealanders") and [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders "Pasifika New Zealanders"). Reflecting this, [New Zealand's culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Zealand "Culture of New Zealand") mainly derives from Māori and early [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_people "British people") settlers but has recently broadened from increased [immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_New_Zealand "Immigration to New Zealand"). [The official languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_New_Zealand "Languages of New Zealand") are English, [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language"), and [New Zealand Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Sign_Language "New Zealand Sign Language"), with the [local dialect of English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_English "New Zealand English") being dominant.

A [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country "Developed country"), New Zealand was the first to introduce a [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage "Minimum wage") and to give [women the right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage_in_New_Zealand "Women's suffrage in New Zealand"). Recognised as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power "Middle power"), New Zealand [ranks very highly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_New_Zealand "International rankings of New Zealand") in international measures of [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index "Human Development Index") and [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_New_Zealand "Human rights in New Zealand") and has one of the lowest levels of [perceived corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_perception_index "Corruption perception index") in the world. It retains [visible levels of inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality_in_New_Zealand "Economic inequality in New Zealand"), including [structural disparities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_discrimination_in_New_Zealand "Structural discrimination in New Zealand") between its Māori and European populations. During the 1980s, New Zealand underwent [major economic changes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogernomics "Rogernomics") that transformed it from a [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism "Protectionism") to a [liberalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization "Economic liberalization") [free-trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade "Free trade") economy. The [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy "Tertiary sector of the economy") dominates the [country's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_New_Zealand "Economy of New Zealand"), followed by the industrial sector, and [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_New_Zealand "Agriculture in New Zealand"); international [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_New_Zealand "Tourism in New Zealand") is also a significant source of revenue. New Zealand and Australia have [a strong relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations "Australia–New Zealand relations") and are considered to share a strong [Trans-Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Tasman "Trans-Tasman") identity, stemming from centuries of British colonisation. The country is part of [multiple international organisations and forums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outline_of_New_Zealand#International_organisation_membership "Outline of New Zealand").

Nationally, legislative authority is vested in an elected, [unicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicameralism "Unicameralism") [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Parliament "New Zealand Parliament"), while executive political power is exercised by the [Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Government "New Zealand Government"), led by the [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_New_Zealand "Prime Minister of New Zealand"), currently [Christopher Luxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Luxon "Christopher Luxon"). [Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III "Charles III") is [the country's king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_New_Zealand "Monarchy of New Zealand") and is represented by the [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand "Governor-General of New Zealand"), [Cindy Kiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cindy_Kiro "Cindy Kiro"). New Zealand is organised into 11 [regional councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_New_Zealand "Regions of New Zealand") and 67 [territorial authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_authorities_of_New_Zealand "Territorial authorities of New Zealand") for [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand "Local government in New Zealand") purposes. The [Realm of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realm_of_New_Zealand "Realm of New Zealand") also includes [Tokelau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokelau "Tokelau") (a [dependent territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependent_territory "Dependent territory")); the [Cook Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands "Cook Islands") and [Niue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niue "Niue") (self-governing states in [free association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associated_state "Associated state") with New Zealand); and the [Ross Dependency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Dependency "Ross Dependency"), which is New Zealand's [territorial claim in Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_claims_in_Antarctica "Territorial claims in Antarctica").

## Etymology

Further information: [New Zealand place names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_place_names "New Zealand place names")

[![Brown square paper with Dutch writing and a thick red, curved line](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Nova_Zeelandia_by_Janssonius%28134121958%29.jpg/250px-Nova_Zeelandia_by_Janssonius%28134121958%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nova_Zeelandia_by_Janssonius(134121958).jpg) Detail from a 1666 map showing the western coastline of _Nova Zeelandia_ (on this map, north is at the bottom)

The first European visitor to New Zealand, Dutch explorer [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman "Abel Tasman"), named the islands _Staten Land_, believing they were part of the _[Staten Landt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isla_de_los_Estados "Isla de los Estados")_ that [Jacob Le Maire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Le_Maire "Jacob Le Maire") had sighted off the southern end of South America.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-StatenLandt-22)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-23) The next year, in 1643, [Hendrik Brouwer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hendrik_Brouwer "Hendrik Brouwer") proved that the South American land was just a small island, and Dutch [cartographers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartographers "Cartographers") subsequently renamed Tasman's discovery _Nova Zeelandia_ from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Latin "Neo-Latin"), after the [Dutch province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_Zeeland "County of Zeeland") of [Zeeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeeland "Zeeland").[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-StatenLandt-22)[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-24) This name was later [anglicised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicisation "Anglicisation") to _New Zealand_.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-25)[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Names-26)

This was written as _Nu Tireni_ in the [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language") (spelt _Nu Tirani_ in Te Tiriti o Waitangi). In 1834, a document written in Māori, "_He Wakaputanga o te Rangatiratanga o Nu Tireni_", was translated into English and became the [Declaration of the Independence of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Independence_of_New_Zealand "Declaration of the Independence of New Zealand"). It was prepared by _Te W(h)akaminenga o Nga Rangatiratanga o Nga Hapu o Nu Tireni_, the [United Tribes of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Tribes_of_New_Zealand "United Tribes of New Zealand"), and a copy was sent to King [William IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_IV "William IV"), who had already acknowledged the flag of the United Tribes of New Zealand and who recognised the declaration in a letter from [Lord Glenelg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Grant,_1st_Baron_Glenelg "Charles Grant, 1st Baron Glenelg").[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-glenelgletter-27)[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalmer200841-28)

_[Aotearoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aotearoa "Aotearoa")_ (pronounced Māori pronunciation:[\[aɔˈtɛaɾɔa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/M%C4%81ori "Help:IPA/Māori")[Play audio](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/00/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav/Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aotearoa_Pronunciation.wav "File:Aotearoa Pronunciation.wav") in Māori and [/ˌaʊtɛəˈroʊ.ə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") in English, often translated as 'land of the long white cloud')[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing200341-29) is the present Māori name for New Zealand; _Aotearoa_ originally referred to just the [North Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Island "North Island"). Māori had no known name for the islands collectively before the arrival of Europeans.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHayMaclaganGordon200872-30) Māori had several traditional names for the two main islands, including _Te Ika-a-Māui_ ('the fish of [Māui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ui_(M%C4%81ori_mythology) "Māui (Māori mythology)")') for the North Island and _Te Waipounamu_ ('the waters of [greenstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounamu "Pounamu")') or _Te Waka o Aoraki_ ('the canoe of [Aoraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aoraki "Aoraki")') for the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island "South Island").[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith20056-31) Early European maps labelled the islands North (North Island), Middle (South Island), and South ( [Stewart Island / Rakiura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stewart_Island_/_Rakiura "Stewart Island / Rakiura")).[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-32)

In 1830, mapmakers began to use "North" and "South" on their maps to distinguish the two largest islands, and by 1907, this was the accepted norm.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Names-26) The [New Zealand Geographic Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Geographic_Board "New Zealand Geographic Board") discovered in 2009 that the names of the North Island and South Island had never been formalised, and names and alternative names were formalised in 2013. This set the names as _North Island_ or _Te Ika-a-Māui_, and _South Island_ or _Te Waipounamu_.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Williamson2013-33) For each island, either its English or Māori name can be used, or both can be used together.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Williamson2013-33) Similarly the Māori and English names for the whole country are sometimes used together ( _Aotearoa New Zealand_);[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-34)[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-35) however, this has no official recognition.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-36)

## History

Main article: [History of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Zealand "History of New Zealand")

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of New Zealand history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_New_Zealand_history "Timeline of New Zealand history").

[![One set of arrows point from Taiwan to Melanesia to Fiji/Samoa and then to the Marquesas Islands. The population then spread, some going south to New Zealand and others going north to Hawai'i. A second set start in southern Asia and end in Melanesia.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Polynesian_Migration.svg/250px-Polynesian_Migration.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Polynesian_Migration.svg) The [Māori people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people "Māori people") descend from [Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians "Polynesians") whose ancestors emigrated from [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan") to [Melanesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesia "Melanesia") between 3000 and 1000 [BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era "Common Era") and then travelled east, reaching the [Society Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Islands "Society Islands") c.1000 CE. After a pause of 200 to 300 years, a new wave of exploration led to the discovery and settlement of New Zealand.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-37)[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-38)[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-39)

### Early settlements

The first people to reach New Zealand were Polynesians in oceangoing _[waka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_(canoe) "Waka (canoe)")_, who are believed to have arrived in several waves between about 1280 and 1350. According to most [Māori oral traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_history "Māori history"), the islands were first discovered by the semi-legendary explorer [Kupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kupe "Kupe") while in pursuit of a giant octopus.[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-40) These traditions hold that Kupe was followed by a great fleet of settlers, who set out from Hawaiki in eastern Polynesia around 1350.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-41) The existence of a single great fleet that settled New Zealand has since been superseded by the belief that the majority of settlement was a planned and deliberate event that occurred over several decades.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-42)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-43)[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-44)[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-45)[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-46)

The exact date of this settlement is unclear, with recent sources favouring settlement in the 14th century. While [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA "Mitochondrial DNA") variability within Māori populations suggests that New Zealand was first settled between 1250 and 1300,[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith20056-31)[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-47)[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-48) no human remains, artefacts or structures can be reliably dated to earlier than the Kaharoa eruption of [Mount Tarawera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Tarawera "Mount Tarawera") around 1314.[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-49) This scenario is also consistent with a debated third line of oral evidence,[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Moon_2013-50) traditional genealogies (_[whakapapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whakapapa "Whakapapa")_) that point to around 1350 as a probable arrival date for several of the [migratory waka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_migration_canoes "Māori migration canoes") (canoes) from which many Māori trace their descent.[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-51)[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-52) Some Māori later migrated to the [Chatham Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands "Chatham Islands"), where they developed their distinct [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori "Moriori") culture;[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-53) a later 1835 invasion by Māori [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi "Iwi") resulted in the massacre and virtual extinction of the Moriori.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-54)

In a hostile 1642 encounter between [Ngāti Tūmatakōkiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_T%C5%ABmatak%C5%8Dkiri "Ngāti Tūmatakōkiri") and [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman "Abel Tasman")'s crew,[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-55)[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith200523-56) four of Tasman's crew members were killed and at least one Māori was hit by [canister shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canister_shot "Canister shot").[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-57) Europeans did not revisit New Zealand until 1769, when British explorer [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook "James Cook") mapped almost the entire coastline.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMein_Smith200523-56) Following Cook, New Zealand was visited by numerous European and North American [whaling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_whaling "History of whaling"), [sealing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_hunting "Seal hunting"), and trading ships. They traded European food, metal tools, weapons, and other goods for timber, Māori food, artefacts, and water.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing2003122-58)

### 19th century

The introduction of the [potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato "Potato") and the [musket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket "Musket") transformed Māori agriculture and warfare. Potatoes provided a reliable food surplus, which enabled longer and more sustained military campaigns.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-59) The resulting intertribal [Musket Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket_Wars "Musket Wars") encompassed over 600 battles between 1801 and 1840, killing 30,000–40,000 Māori.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-60) From the early 19th century, Christian [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary "Missionary") began to settle New Zealand, eventually [converting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion "Religious conversion") most of the Māori population.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-61) The Māori population declined to around 40% of its pre-contact level during the 19th century; introduced diseases were the major factor.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-62)

[![A torn sheet of paper](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Treatyofwaitangi.jpg/250px-Treatyofwaitangi.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Treatyofwaitangi.jpg) The Waitangi sheet from the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi "Treaty of Waitangi")

The [British Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resident_minister "Resident minister") appointed [James Busby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Busby "James Busby") as British Resident to New Zealand in 1832.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Busby-63) His duties, given to him by Governor [Bourke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Bourke "Richard Bourke") in Sydney, were to protect settlers and traders "of good standing", prevent "outrages" against Māori, and apprehend escaped convicts.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Busby-63)[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-64) In 1835, following an announcement of impending French settlement by [Charles de Thierry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Thierry "Charles de Thierry"), the nebulous [United Tribes of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Tribes_of_New_Zealand "United Tribes of New Zealand") sent [a Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/He_Whakaputanga "He Whakaputanga") to King [William IV of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_IV_of_the_United_Kingdom "William IV of the United Kingdom") asking for protection.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Busby-63) The [Colonial Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Office "Colonial Office") sent Captain [William Hobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hobson "William Hobson") to claim British sovereignty and negotiate a treaty with the Māori, prompted by growing unrest, the [New Zealand Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Company "New Zealand Company")'s land-buying survey, and the legal ambiguity of the 1835 Declaration of Independence.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-65) The [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi "Treaty of Waitangi") was first signed in the [Bay of Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Islands "Bay of Islands") on 6 February 1840.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Wilson2009-66) In response to the New Zealand Company's attempts to establish an independent settlement in [Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington "Wellington"),[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-67)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-68) Hobson declared British sovereignty over all of New Zealand on 21 May 1840, even though copies of the treaty were still circulating throughout the country for Māori to sign.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-69) With the signing of the treaty and declaration of sovereignty, the number of immigrants, particularly from the United Kingdom, began to increase.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-70)

New Zealand was administered as a dependency of the [Colony of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_South_Wales "Colony of New South Wales") until becoming a separate [Crown colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_colony "Crown colony"), the [Colony of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_Zealand "Colony of New Zealand"), on 3 May 1841.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-71)[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-72) Armed conflict began between the colonial government and Māori in 1843 with the [Wairau Affray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairau_Affray "Wairau Affray") over land and disagreements over sovereignty. These conflicts, mainly in the North Island, saw thousands of imperial troops and the Royal Navy come to New Zealand, and became known as the [New Zealand Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Wars "New Zealand Wars"). Following these armed conflicts, large areas of [Māori land were confiscated by the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_land_confiscations "New Zealand land confiscations").[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-73)

[![Black and white engraving depicting a crowd of people](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/1863_Meeting_of_Settlers_and_Maoris_at_Hawke%27s_Bay%2C_New_Zealand.jpg/330px-1863_Meeting_of_Settlers_and_Maoris_at_Hawke%27s_Bay%2C_New_Zealand.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1863_Meeting_of_Settlers_and_Maoris_at_Hawke's_Bay,_New_Zealand.jpg) A meeting of European and Māori residents of [Hawke's Bay Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawke's_Bay_Province "Hawke's Bay Province"). Engraving, 1863.

The colony gained a [representative government in 1852](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Constitution_Act_1852 "New Zealand Constitution Act 1852"), and the [first Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_New_Zealand_Parliament "1st New Zealand Parliament") met in 1854.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-G_and_N-74) In 1856 the colony effectively became self-governing, gaining responsibility over all domestic matters, except [native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples "Indigenous peoples") policy, which was granted in the mid-1860s.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-G_and_N-74) Following concerns that the South Island might form a separate colony, premier [Alfred Domett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Domett "Alfred Domett") moved a resolution to transfer the [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_of_New_Zealand "Capital of New Zealand") from Auckland to a locality near [Cook Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Strait "Cook Strait").[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-75)[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-76) Wellington was chosen for its central location, with Parliament officially sitting there for the first time in 1865.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-77)

In 1886, following requests from the government in Wellington concerned about foreign influence in the region, the Secretary of State for Colonies in London reluctantly agreed to annex the volcanic [Kermadec Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermadec_Islands "Kermadec Islands") to New Zealand, islands that were seemingly not of "any possible use...to that colony". As a result, instructions were issued directing the Admiral on the Australian Station to hoist the British flag on the Kermadecs,[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-78) which lie about 1,000km (620mi) northeast of Auckland. Since 1937, the islands have been uninhabited except for about six people at [Raoul Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_Island "Raoul Island") station. These islands put the northern border of New Zealand at 29 degrees South latitude.[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-ENZ1966-79) After the 1982 [UNCLOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNCLOS "UNCLOS"), the islands contributed significantly to New Zealand's [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_New_Zealand "Exclusive economic zone of New Zealand").[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-80)

In 1891, the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Liberal_Party "New Zealand Liberal Party") came to power as the first organised political party.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Liberal-81) The [Liberal Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Government_of_New_Zealand "Liberal Government of New Zealand"), led by [Richard Seddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Seddon "Richard Seddon") for most of its period in office,[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-82) passed many important social and economic measures. In 1893, New Zealand was the first nation in the world to grant all [women the right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage_in_New_Zealand "Women's suffrage in New Zealand")[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Liberal-81) and pioneered the [adoption of compulsory arbitration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Conciliation_and_Arbitration_Act_1894 "Industrial Conciliation and Arbitration Act 1894") between employers and unions in 1894.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-83) The Liberals also guaranteed a minimum wage in 1894, a world first.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-84)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Armistice_Day_Celebrations_at_Municipal_Chambers%2C_1918_%2814666650935%29.jpg/250px-Armistice_Day_Celebrations_at_Municipal_Chambers%2C_1918_%2814666650935%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Armistice_Day_Celebrations_at_Municipal_Chambers,_1918_(14666650935).jpg) Residents of [Dunedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunedin "Dunedin") celebrate the news of the [Armistice of 11 November 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918 "Armistice of 11 November 1918").

### 20th century

In 1907, at the request of the New Zealand Parliament, King [Edward VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_VII "Edward VII") proclaimed New Zealand a [Dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_Zealand "Dominion of New Zealand") within the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire"),[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-85) reflecting its self-governing status.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-86) At the [1926 Imperial Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_Imperial_Conference "1926 Imperial Conference"), the Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom and the Dominions [unanimously declared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1926 "Balfour Declaration of 1926") that all were equal and autonomous members of the British Commonwealth of Nations. In 1947, New Zealand [adopted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_Adoption_Act_1947 "Statute of Westminster Adoption Act 1947") the [Statute of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931 "Statute of Westminster 1931") implementing the declaration, confirming that the [British Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Parliament "British Parliament") could no longer legislate for the country without its consent. The British government's residual legislative powers were later removed by the [Constitution Act 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Act_1986 "Constitution Act 1986"), and final rights of appeal to the [British Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Privy_Council "British Privy Council") were abolished in 2003.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-G_and_N-74)

Early in the 20th century, New Zealand was involved in world affairs, fighting in the [First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_New_Zealand_during_World_War_I "Military history of New Zealand during World War I") and [Second World Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_New_Zealand_during_World_War_II "Military history of New Zealand during World War II")[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-87) and suffering through the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression "Great Depression").[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-88) The depression led to the election of the [first Labour Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Labour_Government_of_New_Zealand "First Labour Government of New Zealand") and the establishment of a comprehensive [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state "Welfare state") and a [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist "Protectionist") economy.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-89) New Zealand experienced increasing prosperity following the Second World War,[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-90) and Māori began to leave their traditional rural life and move to the cities in search of work.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-91) A [Māori protest movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_protest_movement "Māori protest movement") developed, which criticised [Eurocentrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocentrism "Eurocentrism") and worked for greater recognition of [Māori culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_culture "Māori culture") and of the Treaty of Waitangi.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-92) In 1975, the [Waitangi Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Tribunal "Waitangi Tribunal") was set up to investigate alleged breaches of the Treaty, and it was enabled to investigate historic grievances in 1985.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Wilson2009-66) The government has negotiated [settlements of these grievances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi_claims_and_settlements "Treaty of Waitangi claims and settlements") with many iwi,[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-93) although [Māori claims to the foreshore and seabed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_foreshore_and_seabed_controversy "New Zealand foreshore and seabed controversy") proved controversial in the 2000s.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-94)[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-95)

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_New_Zealand "Geography of New Zealand") and [Environment of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_New_Zealand "Environment of New Zealand")

[![Islands of New Zealand as seen from satellite](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/New_Zealand_23_October_2002.jpg/250px-New_Zealand_23_October_2002.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:New_Zealand_23_October_2002.jpg) The snow-capped [Southern Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alps "Southern Alps") dominate the South Island, while the North Island's [Northland Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northland_Peninsula "Northland Peninsula") stretches towards the subtropics.

New Zealand is near the centre of the [water hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_hemisphere "Water hemisphere") and comprises two main islands and [more than 700 smaller islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_New_Zealand "List of islands of New Zealand").[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Walrond1-96) The two main islands (the [North Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Island "North Island"), or _Te Ika-a-Māui_, and the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island "South Island"), or _Te Waipounamu_) are separated by [Cook Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Strait "Cook Strait"), 22 kilometres (14mi) wide at its narrowest point.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-97) Besides the North and South Islands, the five largest inhabited islands are [Stewart Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stewart_Island "Stewart Island") (across the [Foveaux Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foveaux_Strait "Foveaux Strait")), [Chatham Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Island "Chatham Island"), [Great Barrier Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Barrier_Island "Great Barrier Island") (in the [Hauraki Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauraki_Gulf "Hauraki Gulf")),[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-98) [D'Urville Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D'Urville_Island_(New_Zealand) "D'Urville Island (New Zealand)") (in the [Marlborough Sounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlborough_Sounds "Marlborough Sounds"))[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-99) and [Waiheke Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waiheke_Island "Waiheke Island") (about 22km (14mi) from central Auckland).[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-100) The nearest continent is [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia "Australia") to the west north west, with the shortest distance between the Australian and New Zealand mainlands being roughly 1,487 kilometers (924 miles) across the [Tasman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Sea "Tasman Sea"), specifically between [Tasmania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasmania "Tasmania") and [Fiordland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiordland "Fiordland"). The closest point between any territory of both countries is between [Macquarie Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macquarie_Island "Macquarie Island") (Australia) and [Auckland Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Island "Auckland Island") (New Zealand), which are about 618 kilometers (384 miles) apart.

New Zealand is long and narrow—over 1,600 kilometres (990mi) along its north-north-east axis with a maximum width of 400 kilometres (250mi)[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-101)—with about 15,000km (9,300mi) of coastline[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-CIA-102) and a total land area of 268,000 square kilometres (103,500sqmi).[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-103) Because of its far-flung outlying islands and long coastline, the country has extensive marine resources. Its [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_New_Zealand "Exclusive economic zone of New Zealand") is one of the world's largest, covering more than 15 times its land area.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-104)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/NZL-mtcook-peters-lookout.jpg/250px-NZL-mtcook-peters-lookout.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NZL-mtcook-peters-lookout.jpg) [Aoraki / Mount Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aoraki_/_Mount_Cook "Aoraki / Mount Cook") is the highest point in New Zealand, at 3,724 metres.

The South Island is the largest landmass of New Zealand. It is divided along its length by the [Southern Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alps "Southern Alps").[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-105) There are 18 peaks over 3,000 metres (9,800ft), the highest of which is [Aoraki / Mount Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aoraki_/_Mount_Cook "Aoraki / Mount Cook") at 3,724 metres (12,218ft).[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarden200552-106) [Fiordland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiordland "Fiordland")'s steep mountains and deep [fiords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fiords_of_New_Zealand "List of fiords of New Zealand") record the extensive ice age glaciation of this southwestern corner of the South Island.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-107) The North Island is less mountainous but is [marked by volcanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanology_of_New_Zealand "Volcanology of New Zealand").[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-108) The highly active [Taupō Volcanic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taup%C5%8D_Volcanic_Zone "Taupō Volcanic Zone") has formed a large [volcanic plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Island_Volcanic_Plateau "North Island Volcanic Plateau"), punctuated by the North Island's highest mountain, [Mount Ruapehu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Ruapehu "Mount Ruapehu") (2,797 metres (9,177ft)). The plateau also hosts the country's largest lake, [Lake Taupō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Taup%C5%8D "Lake Taupō"),[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Walrond1-96) nestled in the [caldera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caldera "Caldera") of one of the world's most active [supervolcanoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervolcano "Supervolcano").[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-109) New Zealand is prone to [earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_New_Zealand "List of earthquakes in New Zealand").

The country owes its varied topography, and perhaps even its emergence above the waves, to the dynamic boundary it straddles between the [Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Plate "Pacific Plate") and [Indo-Australian Plates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Australian_Plate "Indo-Australian Plate").[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Keith_2009-110) New Zealand is part of [Zealandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealandia "Zealandia"), a [microcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microcontinent "Microcontinent") nearly half the size of Australia that gradually submerged after breaking away from the [Gondwanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana "Gondwana") supercontinent.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-111)[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-112) About 25 million years ago, a shift in [plate tectonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonic "Plate tectonic") movements began to [contort and crumple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaikoura_orogeny "Kaikoura orogeny") the region. This is now most evident in the Southern Alps, formed by [compression of the crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_collision "Continental collision") beside the [Alpine Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_Fault "Alpine Fault"). Elsewhere, the plate boundary involves the [subduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subduction "Subduction") of one plate under the other, producing the [Puysegur Trench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puysegur_Trench "Puysegur Trench") to the south, the [Hikurangi Trough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hikurangi_Trough "Hikurangi Trough") east of the North Island, and the [Kermadec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermadec_Trench "Kermadec Trench") and [Tonga Trenches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonga_Trench "Tonga Trench")[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-113) further north.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Keith_2009-110)

New Zealand, together with Australia, is part of a wider region known as [Australasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasia "Australasia").[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-NZOD-114) It also forms the southwestern extremity of the geographic and ethnographic region called [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia "Polynesia").[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-115) Oceania is a wider region encompassing the [Australian continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_(continent) "Australia (continent)"), New Zealand, and various island countries in the Pacific Ocean that are not included in the [seven-continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent "Continent") model.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-116)

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_New_Zealand "Climate of New Zealand")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/New_Zealand_K%C3%B6ppen.png/250px-New_Zealand_K%C3%B6ppen.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:New_Zealand_K%C3%B6ppen.png)

[Köppen-Geiger climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen-Geiger_climate_classification "Köppen-Geiger climate classification") map of New Zealand

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Christchurch_Botanic_Gardens_in_autumn.jpg/250px-Christchurch_Botanic_Gardens_in_autumn.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Christchurch_Botanic_Gardens_in_autumn.jpg)

Autumn in the [Christchurch Botanic Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch_Botanic_Gardens "Christchurch Botanic Gardens")

New Zealand's climate is predominantly temperate [maritime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate "Oceanic climate") ( [Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification"): Cfb), with mean annual temperatures ranging from 10°C (50°F) in the south to 16°C (61°F) in the north.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Mullan2009-117) Historical [maxima and minima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxima_and_minima "Maxima and minima") are 42.4°C (108.32°F) in [Rangiora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangiora "Rangiora"), [Canterbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canterbury_Region "Canterbury Region") and −25.6°C (−14.08°F) in [Ranfurly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranfurly,_New_Zealand "Ranfurly, New Zealand"), [Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otago "Otago").[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-niwa-118) Conditions vary sharply across regions, from extremely wet on the [West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast,_New_Zealand "West Coast, New Zealand") of the South Island to [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate "Semi-arid climate") in [Central Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Otago "Central Otago") and the [Mackenzie Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mackenzie_Basin "Mackenzie Basin") of inland Canterbury and [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropical "Subtropical") in [Northland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northland_Peninsula "Northland Peninsula").[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-119)[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-120)

Of the seven largest cities, [Christchurch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch "Christchurch") is the driest, receiving on average only 618 millimetres (24.3in) of rain per year. Wellington is the wettest, receiving almost twice that amount.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-121) Auckland, Wellington and Christchurch all receive a yearly average of more than 2,000 hours of sunshine. The southern and southwestern parts of the South Island have a cooler and cloudier climate, with around 1,400–1,600 hours. The northern and northeastern parts of the South Island are the sunniest areas of the country and receive about 2,400–2,500 hours.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-122)

Snow generally falls from June to October, although [cold snaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_snap "Cold snap") can occur outside of those months.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-123) Snowfall is common in the eastern and southern parts of the South Island and mountain areas across the country.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Mullan2009-117)

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Biodiversity of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_of_New_Zealand "Biodiversity of New Zealand")

[![Kiwi among sticks](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TeTuatahianui.jpg/250px-TeTuatahianui.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TeTuatahianui.jpg) The endemic flightless [kiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwi_(bird) "Kiwi (bird)") is a [national icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_New_Zealand "National symbols of New Zealand").

New Zealand's [geographic isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographic_isolation "Geographic isolation") for 80 million years[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-125) and island [biogeography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogeography "Biogeography") has influenced evolution of the country's species of [animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_New_Zealand "Fauna of New Zealand"), [fungi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fungi_of_New_Zealand "Fungi of New Zealand") and [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_of_New_Zealand "Flora of New Zealand"). Physical isolation has caused biological isolation, resulting in a dynamic evolutionary ecology with examples of distinctive plants and animals as well as populations of widespread species.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-126)[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-127) The flora and fauna of New Zealand were originally thought to have originated from New Zealand's fragmentation off from Gondwana; however, more recent evidence postulates species resulted from dispersal.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-128)

About 82% of New Zealand's indigenous [vascular plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vascular_plant "Vascular plant") are [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemism "Endemism"), covering 1,944 species across 65 [genera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genus "Genus").[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FAQ-129)[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-NZPCN_2006-130) The number of fungi recorded from New Zealand, including lichen-forming species, is not known, nor is the proportion of those fungi which are endemic, but one estimate suggests there are about 2,300 species of lichen-forming fungi in New Zealand[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-FAQ-129) and 40% of these are endemic.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-131) The two main types of forest are those dominated by broadleaf trees with emergent [podocarps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podocarp "Podocarp"), or by [southern beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nothofagus "Nothofagus") in cooler climates.[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-132) The remaining vegetation types consist of grasslands, the majority of which are [tussock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tussock_grasslands_of_New_Zealand "Tussock grasslands of New Zealand").[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-133)

[![An artist's rendition of a Haast's eagle attacking two moa](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Giant_Haasts_eagle_attacking_New_Zealand_moa.jpg/250px-Giant_Haasts_eagle_attacking_New_Zealand_moa.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Giant_Haasts_eagle_attacking_New_Zealand_moa.jpg) The giant [Haast's eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haast's_eagle "Haast's eagle") died out when Māori hunted its main prey, the [moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moa "Moa"), to extinction.

Before humans arrived, an estimated 80% of the land was covered in forest, with only [high alpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_line "Tree line"), wet, infertile and volcanic areas without trees.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-134) Massive [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_New_Zealand "Deforestation in New Zealand") occurred after humans arrived, with around half the forest cover lost to fire after Polynesian settlement.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-135) Much of the remaining forest fell after European settlement, being logged or cleared to make room for pastoral farming, leaving forest occupying only 23% of the land in 1997.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-136)

The forests were dominated by [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birds_of_New_Zealand "Birds of New Zealand"), and the lack of mammalian predators led to some, like the [kiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwi_(bird) "Kiwi (bird)"), [kākāpō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81k%C4%81p%C5%8D "Kākāpō"), [weka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weka "Weka") and [takahē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takah%C4%93 "Takahē"), evolving [flightlessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flightless_bird "Flightless bird").[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-137) The arrival of humans, associated changes to habitat, and the introduction of [rats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_rat "Polynesian rat"), ferrets and other mammals led to the [extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Zealand_animals_extinct_in_the_Holocene "List of New Zealand animals extinct in the Holocene") of many bird species, including [large birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megafauna "Megafauna") like the [moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moa "Moa") and [Haast's eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haast's_eagle "Haast's eagle").[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Holdaway2009-138)[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-139) The forests of the country are home to many endemic birds including [kererū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerer%C5%AB "Kererū"), [New Zealand falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_falcon "New Zealand falcon"), [kākā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81k%C4%81 "Kākā"), [red-crowned parakeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red-crowned_parakeet "Red-crowned parakeet"), [Rifleman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifleman_(bird) "Rifleman (bird)"), [tūī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%AB%C4%AB "Tūī"), [New Zealand fantail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_fantail "New Zealand fantail"), [kōkako](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dkako "Kōkako"), [saddleback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddleback_(bird) "Saddleback (bird)"), [stitchbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stitchbird "Stitchbird"), and [New Zealand fernbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_fernbird "New Zealand fernbird").[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-140) The shorelines are inhabited by many endemic shorebirds including [black stilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_stilt "Black stilt"), [variable oystercatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variable_oystercatcher "Variable oystercatcher"), [shore plover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shore_plover "Shore plover"), [spotted shag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotted_shag "Spotted shag"), and [New Zealand king shag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_king_shag "New Zealand king shag"). Due to the isolation of the islands, and historic lack of predators the nation is home to countless breeding seabirds including [black-billed gull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-billed_gull "Black-billed gull"), [northern royal albatross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_royal_albatross "Northern royal albatross"), [white-faced storm petrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-faced_storm_petrel "White-faced storm petrel"), [grey-faced petrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey-faced_petrel "Grey-faced petrel"), [Fairy prion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairy_prion "Fairy prion"), and [Australasian gannet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasian_gannet "Australasian gannet").[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-141)

Other indigenous animals are represented by reptiles ( [tuatara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuatara "Tuatara"), [skinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skink "Skink") and [geckos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_geckos_of_New_Zealand "List of geckos of New Zealand")), frogs,[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-142) such as the protected endangered [Hamilton's Frog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton's_Frog "Hamilton's Frog"), [spiders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiders_of_New_Zealand "Spiders of New Zealand"),[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-143) insects ([wētā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C4%93t%C4%81 "Wētā")),[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-144) and snails.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-145) Some, such as the tuatara, are so unique that they have been called [living fossils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_fossil "Living fossil").[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-146) Three species of bats ( [one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_greater_short-tailed_bat "New Zealand greater short-tailed bat") since extinct) were the only sign of native land mammals in New Zealand until the 2006 discovery of bones from [a unique, mouse-sized land mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Bathans_mammal "Saint Bathans mammal") at least 16 million years old.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-147)[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-148) Marine mammals are abundant, with almost half the world's [cetaceans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cetacea "Cetacea") (whales, dolphins, and [porpoises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porpoise "Porpoise")) and large numbers of [fur seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_seal "Fur seal") reported in New Zealand waters.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-149) Many seabirds breed in New Zealand, a third of them unique to the country.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-150) More [penguin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_birds_of_New_Zealand#Penguins "List of birds of New Zealand") species are found in New Zealand than in any other country, with 13 of the world's 18 penguin species.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-151)

Since human arrival, almost half of the country's vertebrate species have become extinct, including at least 51 birds, three frogs, three lizards, one freshwater fish, and one bat. Others are endangered or have had their range severely reduced.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Holdaway2009-138) New Zealand conservationists have pioneered several methods to help threatened wildlife recover, including island sanctuaries, pest control, wildlife translocation, fostering, and ecological [restoration of islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_restoration "Island restoration") and other [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_New_Zealand "Protected areas of New Zealand").[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-152)[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-153)[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-154)[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-155)

## Government and politics

Main articles: [New Zealand Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Government "New Zealand Government") and [Politics of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_New_Zealand "Politics of New Zealand")

See also: [International rankings of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_New_Zealand "International rankings of New Zealand")

[![The King wearing a pinstripe suit](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/King_Charles_III_%28July_2023%29.jpg/120px-King_Charles_III_%28July_2023%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:King_Charles_III_(July_2023).jpg)

[Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III "Charles III"),

[King of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_New_Zealand "Monarchy of New Zealand")

[![A woman wearing medals](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Dame_Cindy_Kiro_Official_Photo_May_2023_%28cropped%29.jpg/120px-Dame_Cindy_Kiro_Official_Photo_May_2023_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dame_Cindy_Kiro_Official_Photo_May_2023_(cropped).jpg)

[Cindy Kiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cindy_Kiro "Cindy Kiro"), [Governor-General of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand "Governor-General of New Zealand")

[![A smiling man wearing a dark business suit and tie](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/LUXON%2C_Christopher_-_Botany_%28cropped%29.png/120px-LUXON%2C_Christopher_-_Botany_%28cropped%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LUXON,_Christopher_-_Botany_(cropped).png)

[Christopher Luxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Luxon "Christopher Luxon"), [Prime Minister of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_New_Zealand "Prime Minister of New Zealand")

New Zealand is a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy "Constitutional monarchy") with a [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy "Parliamentary democracy"),[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-GG_constitution-156) although [its constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Zealand "Constitution of New Zealand") is [not codified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncodified_constitution "Uncodified constitution").[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Economist_factsheet-157) [Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III "Charles III") is the [King of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_New_Zealand "King of New Zealand")[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-158) and thus the [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state "Head of state").[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-159) The king is represented by the [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand "Governor-General of New Zealand"), whom he appoints on the [advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_(constitutional) "Advice (constitutional)") of the [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_New_Zealand "Prime Minister of New Zealand").[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-160) The governor-general can exercise [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown "The Crown")'s [prerogative powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prerogative_powers "Prerogative powers"), such as reviewing cases of injustice and making appointments of [ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministers_in_the_New_Zealand_Government "Ministers in the New Zealand Government"), ambassadors, and other key public officials,[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-161) and in rare situations, the [reserve powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_power "Reserve power") (e.g. the power to dissolve Parliament or refuse the [royal assent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_assent "Royal assent") of a [bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_(law) "Bill (law)") into law).[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-reserve_Powers-162) The powers of the monarch and the governor-general are limited by constitutional constraints and cannot normally be exercised without the advice of ministers.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-reserve_Powers-162)

The [New Zealand Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Parliament "New Zealand Parliament") holds [legislative power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_power "Legislative power") and consists of the king and the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_House_of_Representatives "New Zealand House of Representatives").[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-parliament_facts-163) It also included an upper house, the [Legislative Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Legislative_Council "New Zealand Legislative Council"), until this was abolished in 1950.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-parliament_facts-163) The [supremacy of parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacy_of_parliament "Supremacy of parliament") over the Crown and other government institutions was established in England by the [Bill of Rights 1689](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_of_Rights_1689 "Bill of Rights 1689") and has been ratified as law in New Zealand.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-parliament_facts-163) The House of Representatives is democratically elected, and a government is formed from the party or [coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_government "Coalition government") with the majority of seats. If no majority is formed, a [minority government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_government "Minority government") can be formed if support from other parties during [confidence and supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_and_supply "Confidence and supply") votes is assured.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-parliament_facts-163) The governor-general appoints ministers under advice from the prime minister, who is by [convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_convention_(political_custom) "Constitutional convention (political custom)") the [parliamentary leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_leader "Parliamentary leader") of the governing party or coalition.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-164) [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_New_Zealand "Cabinet of New Zealand"), formed by ministers and led by the prime minister, is the highest policy-making body in government and responsible for deciding significant government actions.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-165) Members of Cabinet make major decisions collectively and are therefore [collectively responsible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_collective_responsibility "Cabinet collective responsibility") for the consequences of these decisions.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-166) The 42nd and current prime minister, since 27 November 2023, is [Christopher Luxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Luxon "Christopher Luxon").[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-167)

[![A block of buildings fronted by a large statue.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Seddon_Statue_in_Parliament_Grounds.jpg/250px-Seddon_Statue_in_Parliament_Grounds.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Seddon_Statue_in_Parliament_Grounds.jpg) A statue of [Richard Seddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Seddon "Richard Seddon"), the " [Beehive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beehive_(New_Zealand) "Beehive (New Zealand)")" (Executive Wing), and [Parliament House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_House,_Wellington "Parliament House, Wellington") (right), in Parliament Grounds, [Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington "Wellington")

A [parliamentary general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_New_Zealand "Elections in New Zealand") must be called no later than three years after the previous election.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-168) Almost all general elections between [1853](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1853_New_Zealand_general_election "1853 New Zealand general election") and [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_New_Zealand_general_election "1993 New Zealand general election") were held under the [first-past-the-post voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting "First-past-the-post voting") system.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-169) Since the [1996 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_New_Zealand_general_election "1996 New Zealand general election"), a form of [proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation "Proportional representation") called [mixed-member proportional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-member_proportional "Mixed-member proportional") (MMP) has been used.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Economist_factsheet-157) Under the MMP system, each person has two votes; one is for a candidate standing in the voter's [electorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_electorates "New Zealand electorates"), and the other is for a party. Based on the 2018 census data, there are 72 electorates (which include seven [Māori electorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_electorates "Māori electorates") in which only Māori can optionally vote),[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-170) and the remaining 48 of the 120 seats are assigned so that representation in Parliament reflects the party vote, with the threshold that a party must win at least one electorate or 5% of the total party vote before it is eligible for a seat.[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-171) Elections since the 1930s have been dominated by two [political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_New_Zealand "List of political parties in New Zealand"), [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_National_Party "New Zealand National Party") and [Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Labour_Party "New Zealand Labour Party"). More parties have been represented in Parliament since the introduction of MMP.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-172)

[New Zealand's judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_New_Zealand "Judiciary of New Zealand"), headed by the [chief justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_New_Zealand "Chief Justice of New Zealand"),[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-173) includes the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_New_Zealand "Supreme Court of New Zealand"), [Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_of_New_Zealand "Court of Appeal of New Zealand"), the [High Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Court_of_New_Zealand "High Court of New Zealand"), and subordinate courts.[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-174) Judges and judicial officers are appointed non-politically and under strict rules regarding tenure to help maintain [judicial independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence "Judicial independence").[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-Economist_factsheet-157) This theoretically allows the judiciary to interpret the law based solely on the legislation enacted by Parliament without other influences on their decisions.[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-175)

New Zealand is identified as one of the world's most stable and well-governed states.[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-176)As of 2023,[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=New_Zealand&action=edit) the country is ranked second in the strength of its democratic institutions,[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-177) and third in government transparency and [lack of corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_New_Zealand "Corruption in New Zealand").[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-178) [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_New_Zealand "LGBT rights in New Zealand") in the nation are also recognised as among the most tolerant in Oceania.[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-179) New Zealand ranks highly for civic participation in the political process, with 82% [voter turnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_turnout "Voter turnout") during recent general elections, compared to an [OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OECD "OECD") average of 69%.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-180) But this is untrue of local council elections; a historically low 36% of eligible New Zealanders voted in the [2022 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_New_Zealand_local_elections "2022 New Zealand local elections"), compared with an already low 42% turnout in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_New_Zealand_local_elections "2019 New Zealand local elections").[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-181)[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-182)[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-183) A 2017 [human rights report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_Reports_on_Human_Rights_Practices "Country Reports on Human Rights Practices") by the [United States Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_State "United States Department of State") noted that the New Zealand government generally [respected the rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_New_Zealand "Human rights in New Zealand") of individuals, but voiced concerns regarding the social status of the Māori population.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-184) The [New Zealand Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Commission "Human Rights Commission") has asserted that there is strong, consistent evidence that [structural discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_discrimination_in_New_Zealand "Structural discrimination in New Zealand") is a real and ongoing socioeconomic issue.[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-185) One example of structural inequality in New Zealand can be seen in the criminal justice system. According to the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_(New_Zealand) "Ministry of Justice (New Zealand)"), Māori are overrepresented, comprising 45% of New Zealanders convicted of crimes and 53% of those imprisoned, while only being 16.5% of the population.[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-186)[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand#cite_note-187)

### Regions and external territories

Main articles: [Regions of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_New_Zealand "Regions of New Zealand") and [Realm of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realm_of_New_Zealand "Realm of New Zealand")

[![A map of New Zealand divided into regions and territorial authorities with labels](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/NZ_Regional_Councils_and_Territorial_Authorities_2017.svg/250px-NZ_Regional_Councils_and_Territorial_Authorities_2017.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NZ_Regional_Councils_and_Territorial_Authorities_2017.svg) Map of regions (coloured) and territorial authorities (outlined) in New Zealand

Under legislation passed in London, New Zealand was divided into six [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_New_Zealand "Provinces of New Zealand") in 1853.
